I guess... what really scared me enough to come in today is that I just keep thinking it would be such a relief if I just didn't wake up. \n\nWhich is strange to think about, because I used to have this terrible time sleeping back when I lived in my old apartment on Elm Street. The radiator there used to clank all night long—just bang, bang, bang. I asked the landlord to fix it, oh, a dozen times, but he never did. He was a cheap man, always wearing those same scuffed brown shoes. I remember thinking I should just learn to fix it myself, but I never was much good at mechanical things after I left school. I tried to fix a toaster once and nearly blew a fuse for the whole building. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, about waking up. I haven't actually tried to hurt myself or anything like that. I wouldn't do that. It's just... having that thought, about how nice it would be to just stay asleep forever... it just keeps popping into my head, and it spooked me enough to finally come see somebody.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not wanting to wake up?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been about four months now. It all started right around the time my aunt came to live with us. Which, honestly, has been quite an adjustment. She brought all these boxes with her, just towering stacks of them, filled with old porcelain figurines. Little dogs, mostly. Poodles and terriers with these tiny, painted-on eyes that always seem to be staring right at you. I told her we didn't have the shelf space, especially not in the living room where the good sunlight comes in, but she insisted. She said sunlight fades the paint, but I read in a magazine once—or maybe it was on a television program—that dust is actually worse for porcelain than sunlight. Dust gets into the little crevices, you see. I try to keep up with the dusting, I really do, but it just feels like such a chore these days. Everything feels like a chore. \n\nBut yes, about four months... since she moved in. That's when those thoughts started creeping in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before your aunt moved in?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I used to just love my watercolors, I really did. I'd always look forward to getting them out. I have this beautiful set that came in a lovely metal tin, though the little clasp on it is awfully stiff. My fingers don't work quite the way they used to, especially when it rains. And heavens, it has been raining so much this month. The man on the local news said it's the wettest spring we've had in a decade. I always worry about the basement flooding when it rains like this, because back in the late nineties we had a terrible flood and I lost three of my good winter coats. Just completely ruined them with mildew. I had to throw them all out in big black garbage bags. \n\nBut... oh, what was I saying? Right, about enjoying things. Yes, I used to look forward to the watercolors, and my dance class, and going for walks with the ladies in the neighborhood. We used to walk the whole perimeter of the park together. Martha always tries to race everyone since she got her new hip, which is just silly at our age, but she means well. \n\nLately, though... I don't know. I just leave my painting things sitting right there on the table. The water cup just sits there drying out. I don't go to dance, and I don't go walking. I just can't seem to make myself care about any of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a moment ago, yes, I really have. I don't touch my paints or go to my classes. The interest is just... gone. I don't even care to go down to the grocery store much anymore, to be honest. \n\nI used to really like picking out the fresh fruit at that little market on the corner. They always had those lovely Georgia peaches in the summer. My mother used to make the most wonderful peach cobbler when I was a girl. She had this old cast iron skillet that weighed an absolute ton, and she'd bake the cobbler right in there. You had to use a special thick mitt just to take it out of the oven, otherwise you'd burn your hand right off. My brother actually did burn his hand on it once, had a blister the size of a quarter on his thumb for weeks. He was always getting into things he shouldn't, climbing fences and tearing the knees right out of his trousers. My mother was always patching them up with these little iron-on squares... \n\nBut, um, anyway... yes. I've lost interest in all of it. Just like I said.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about these thoughts of not wanting to wake up?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Well... yes. It just eats at me. You see, when I get so tired, or when I get annoyed with my aunt—which is happening more and more with all those boxes of hers in the way—I start thinking I'm just a bad woman. I really do. I feel like I'm bringing shame on my family for not being more grateful that we have each other. \n\nMy family was always very strict about gratitude. We had to write thank-you notes for absolutely everything when I was a girl. Even if it was just a plain pair of white socks from the Sears catalog. Do you remember how thick the Sears catalog used to be? You could use it as a booster seat. My mother actually did use it as a booster seat for my cousin Betty when she came to visit. Betty was always so small for her age. She ended up marrying a man who was nearly six foot four, if you can believe it! They looked so funny standing next to each other at the altar. He wore this awful mustard-colored tuxedo, which I suppose was popular back then, but it just clashed terribly with the bridesmaids' dresses. They were a lovely seafoam green, made of this shiny taffeta material that rustled every time you took a step. I always loved taffeta, even though it's the devil to iron... \n\nBut... oh dear, I'm rambling again, aren't I? What was the question? Oh, the guilt. Yes. I just feel so ashamed of myself for being so ungrateful about my aunt.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel pleasure when you do things that you used to enjoy, even if you don't feel like doing them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. Even if I force myself to sit down with my watercolors, it just feels like... a chore, I suppose. There's no spark there anymore. I used to get such a thrill when the colors blended just right on the paper, especially the blues and the greens. I had this one tube of cerulean blue that was just magnificent. I bought it at this little art supply shop downtown that's been closed for... oh, it must be five years now. The owner was a lovely man named Mr. Henderson. He always wore these little half-moon glasses perched right on the end of his nose, and he had a ginger cat that would sleep right on top of the cash register. It was the fattest cat I've ever seen in my life, just a massive thing. Mr. Henderson said it ate nothing but tuna fish and heavy cream, which I told him was terrible for the poor creature's heart, but he just laughed. He had a very deep, booming laugh that you wouldn't expect from such a small man. He actually reminded me a bit of my uncle Arthur, the one who used to play the accordion at all the family reunions...\n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry. No, I don't get any pleasure from it. Not the painting, not the dance class, not the walks. I just leave my things sitting there and I really can't make myself care about any of it, no matter how hard I try.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Well, my husband actually mentioned that the other day. He says I move like everything takes extra effort now. Even when I go to the market, I just... I stand there for the longest time before picking up what I came for. Like the other day, I was standing in front of the canned tomatoes. There used to be just two kinds, whole or crushed, but now they have diced, fire-roasted, with basil, with garlic... it's just too much. I remember when my grandmother used to can her own tomatoes. She had this huge garden out back, and in late August, her kitchen would just be covered in mason jars. She'd boil them in this enormous aluminum pot that took up two burners on the stove. The whole house smelled like vinegar and hot glass. She'd always make me write the labels because she said my handwriting was the neatest. I used to practice my cursive for hours in this little spiral notebook with a picture of a kitten on the front. I think I bought it at the Woolworth's that used to be on Main Street. They had a lunch counter there where you could get a grilled cheese sandwich and a cherry Coke for practically nothing. The lady who worked the counter was named Marge, and she always wore her hair in this towering beehive...\n\nBut, um, what were we saying? Oh, yes. Moving slowly. Yes, I suppose I do. Everything just feels so heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Weight? Oh, no, I haven't really noticed any change. My clothes still fit the same, I suppose. Though I haven't been dressing up much lately. I mostly just wear my old housecoats. I have this one flannel housecoat that I've had for... oh, it must be fifteen years. It's a pale yellow with little blue cornflowers on it. It used to be so soft, but it's gotten a bit scratchy from being washed so many times. I always wash it with that powdered detergent, the kind that comes in the big cardboard box. My mother always said powder was better than liquid, but she was very particular about laundry. She used to hang everything out on the line, even in the dead of winter. She said the freezing air made the sheets smell fresher. I remember helping her pin them up, and my fingers would get so numb I could barely squeeze the clothespins. We had those wooden clothespins, the ones with the little metal springs. My brother used to take them apart and use the springs to build these little catapults. He was always building things. He wanted to be an engineer, but then he ended up working at the post office... \n\nBut, no, my weight hasn't really changed. I just don't have much of an appetite, but I haven't lost any weight that I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose I do. Especially when it comes to my aunt. She needs a lot of help, and sometimes I just feel so tired, or I get annoyed with her. And then I start thinking I'm just a terrible, bad woman. I feel like I'm bringing shame on my whole family for not being more grateful and patient. Family is so important, you know. My aunt, she used to bake these incredible pies for every family gathering. Rhubarb, mostly, because she grew it right along the side of her garage. She had this huge patch of it, and the leaves were as big as elephant ears. I used to hide under them when I was a little girl playing hide-and-seek with my cousins. My cousin Tommy was always the seeker because he was the oldest, but he was terrible at it. He'd just wander around looking at bugs. He was obsessed with caterpillars. He used to keep them in these old mayonnaise jars with holes punched in the lid. He had this one big fuzzy one that he named Bartholomew... \n\nBut, oh dear, I'm wandering again. Yes, I just... I feel so guilty and worthless when I get frustrated with her. I should be better.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about actually doing something to make yourself not wake up?\n\nPatient: Doing something? Oh, no, I haven't tried to hurt myself. But... I do keep thinking it would be such a relief if I just didn't wake up at all. Honestly, that's what scared me enough to finally come in here to see you. Just the thought of staying asleep. I have this very old mattress, you see. It's one we bought back in nineteen-ninety-something. We had it delivered in this big truck, and the delivery men had such a time getting it up the stairs because our staircase has that sharp turn right at the landing. We have this little oak table on the landing where I keep a spider plant. Spider plants are so easy to take care of, they just shoot off those little babies on the stems. I used to snip them off and root them in little glass jars of water on the kitchen windowsill. My kitchen window faces east, so it gets all that bright morning light. The glass jars would catch the sun and make these little rainbows on the linoleum floor. Our old cat, Barnaby—he was a big orange tabby with a white patch on his nose—he used to chase those little rainbows for hours until he'd just tire himself out and fall asleep right there on the rug... \n\nWait, what was I... oh. Yes. I haven't actually tried to do anything. It just scared me that I wanted to just stay asleep like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or restless, or do you feel like you're moving in slow motion most of the time?\n\nPatient: Well, my husband says I move like everything just takes extra effort now. I don't feel restless, really, just... heavy, I suppose. He noticed it especially when we go to the market. He says I'll just stand there for entirely too long before picking up what I came for. We usually go to that little market down on 4th Street, the one with the faded green awning? They used to have the absolute best tomatoes in the summer, those big heavy beefsteak ones. My husband just loves a tomato sandwich with a little mayonnaise and black pepper on toasted white bread. We used to buy our bread from the bakery right next door to the market, but they closed down last spring. The owner, Mr. Abernathy, he finally retired and moved down to Florida to live with his daughter. She just had twins, a boy and a girl, and he was so excited to be a grandfather. He always wore these little flour-dusted aprons and he'd give the neighborhood kids free sugar cookies on Tuesdays... \n\nOh, excuse me, I'm rambling again. But yes, I suppose I do feel like I'm in slow motion. Just standing at the shelves, trying to gather the energy to reach out and grab a can of soup.\n\nDoctor: Have you made any plans for how you would make yourself not wake up?\n\nPatient: No, no plans. Like I was saying before... I haven't tried to hurt myself or figure out a way to do it. It's just that thought, that feeling that it would be such a relief to just... not wake up. And that's really what scared me enough to finally come in here to see you. Just the thought of it. Because usually, I'm the one taking care of everything, you know? I have to make sure my husband's shirts are pressed for church on Sundays. We go to the First Presbyterian over on Elm Street. It has those beautiful stained glass windows that they had restored back in 2005. They brought in these specialists all the way from Boston just to fix the lead lining. It took them three months, and during that time we had to hold services in the fellowship hall in the basement. The basement always smelled a little bit like old coffee and floor wax. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, he used this industrial wax that came in these big metal tins, and he'd buff the floors every Friday afternoon so they'd be shining for Sunday... \n\nOh, I'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? But no, to answer your question, no plans. Just that scary thought of wanting the relief of not waking up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not restless. Like I mentioned a moment ago, my husband says I move like everything takes extra effort now. I don't feel like I need to fidget or move around. It's more like... I'm just so heavy. Even at the market, I'll just stand there for entirely too long before picking up what I came for. It's like my arms are made of lead. Lead is such a heavy metal, isn't it? My father used to work in a factory that made those lead pipes for plumbing, back before they realized how dangerous it was. He'd come home smelling like hot metal and grease, and his hands were always so rough. He used this special pumice soap to wash up at the kitchen sink. It came in this little green tub and it had real grit in it, like sand. My mother hated it because it always left a ring around the porcelain, and she was so particular about keeping the sink sparkling white. She'd scrub it with baking soda and a little bit of vinegar. It would fizz up so nicely... \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, the restlessness. No, I don't feel restless at all. Just very, very slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. I know you've asked about that a few times now, but it's actually quite the opposite. Like I was telling you before, my husband always points out that I move like every little thing takes so much extra effort. He says I'm just so slow. Like when we're at the market, I'll just stand there staring at the shelves for entirely too long before I finally pick up what we need. We usually go on Tuesday mornings because they get their fresh produce deliveries on Mondays. They have this young boy who unloads the trucks, he can't be more than nineteen. He reminds me so much of my nephew, David. David was always such a hard worker, even when he was little he'd help his father chop wood out back. They had this beautiful old oak tree that got struck by lightning during a terrible storm back in '98, I think it was. The thunder was so loud it rattled the good china in my mother's hutch. That hutch was solid cherry wood, passed down from my grandmother... \n\nOh, I'm doing it again. I'm sorry. But no, no pacing or constant motion. Just very slow and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently that you've noticed?\n\nPatient: Weight? No, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any change in how my clothes fit or anything. I still wear the same dresses I've had for years. My favorite one is this navy blue floral print I bought for my niece's graduation back in... oh, it must have been 2012. She graduated from the state university with a degree in education. She's a second-grade teacher now, over at the elementary school on Maple Drive. They just built a new playground there last summer with those soft rubber mats underneath the swings instead of woodchips. Woodchips always seemed so dangerous to me, you know? You could get a splinter right through your shoe if you weren't careful. When I was a little girl, I got a terrible splinter in my heel from walking barefoot on our back porch. My mother had to use a sterilized needle to dig it out, and it stung so badly. She put iodine on it afterward, and it stained my skin orange for days...\n\nOh dear, I've wandered off again. But no, to answer your question, my weight has been about the same. I haven't lost any.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And that's really what scared me enough to finally come in here to see you. Just the thought of it. Because usually, I'm the one taking care of everything, you know? I have to make sure my husband's shirts are pressed for church on Sundays. We go to the First Presbyterian over on Elm Street. It has those beautiful stained glass windows that they had restored back in 2005. They brought in these specialists all the way from Boston just to fix the lead lining. It took them three months, and during that time we had to hold services in the fellowship hall in the basement. The basement always smelled a little bit like old coffee and floor wax. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, he used this industrial wax that came in these big metal tins, and he'd buff the floors every Friday afternoon so they'd be shining for Sunday... \n\nOh, I'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? But no, to answer your question, no plans. Just that scary thought of wanting the relief of not waking up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not restless. My father used to work in a factory that made those lead pipes for plumbing, back before they realized how dangerous it was. He'd come home smelling like hot metal and grease, and his hands were always so rough. He used this special pumice soap to wash up at the kitchen sink. It came in this little green tub and it had real grit in it, like sand. My mother hated it because it always left a ring around the porcelain, and she was so particular about keeping the sink sparkling white. She'd scrub it with baking soda and a little bit of vinegar. It would fizz up so nicely... \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, the restlessness. No, I don't feel restless at all. Just very, very slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. Well, no, not a normal pace. I think I've mentioned this a couple times now, but my husband says I move like everything takes extra effort. Like I'm just so slow. Even at the market, I'll just stand there entirely too long before I finally pick up what I came for. The market gets so chilly, too, right in that produce section. They keep the air conditioning blasting over the lettuce to keep it crisp, I suppose. I always have to bring a cardigan. I have this lovely cream-colored one I knitted myself, took me almost a year to finish because my arthritis was acting up so badly that winter. The yarn was a blend of merino wool and silk, very soft, but quite expensive. I bought it at that little craft store downtown, the one that used to be a bakery. You can still smell the yeast and vanilla sometimes if you go in early enough. They used to make the most wonderful bear claws there, with real almond paste... 

Oh, I'm sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? But no, I don't feel like I'm moving at a normal pace at all. Just very slow and heavy.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.4, F32.5, F32.8, F32.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello doctor. I guess... what really scared me enough to come in today is that I just keep thinking it would be such a relief if I just didn't wake up. \n\nWhich is strange to think about, because I used to have this terrible time sleeping back when I lived in my old apartment on Elm Street. The radiator there used to clank all night long—just bang, bang, bang. I asked the landlord to fix it, oh, a dozen times, but he never did. He was a cheap man, always wearing those same scuffed brown shoes. I remember thinking I should just learn to fix it myself, but I never was much good at mechanical things after I left school. I tried to fix a toaster once and nearly blew a fuse for the whole building. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, about waking up. I haven't actually tried to hurt myself or anything like that. I wouldn't do that. It's just... having that thought, about how nice it would be to just stay asleep forever... it just keeps popping into my head, and it spooked me enough to finally come see somebody.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not wanting to wake up?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been about four months now. It all started right around the time my aunt came to live with us. Which, honestly, has been quite an adjustment. She brought all these boxes with her, just towering stacks of them, filled with old porcelain figurines. Little dogs, mostly. Poodles and terriers with these tiny, painted-on eyes that always seem to be staring right at you. I told her we didn't have the shelf space, especially not in the living room where the good sunlight comes in, but she insisted. She said sunlight fades the paint, but I read in a magazine once—or maybe it was on a television program—that dust is actually worse for porcelain than sunlight. Dust gets into the little crevices, you see. I try to keep up with the dusting, I really do, but it just feels like such a chore these days. Everything feels like a chore. \n\nBut yes, about four months... since she moved in. That's when those thoughts started creeping in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before your aunt moved in?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I used to just love my watercolors, I really did. I'd always look forward to getting them out. I have this beautiful set that came in a lovely metal tin, though the little clasp on it is awfully stiff. My fingers don't work quite the way they used to, especially when it rains. And heavens, it has been raining so much this month. The man on the local news said it's the wettest spring we've had in a decade. I always worry about the basement flooding when it rains like this, because back in the late nineties we had a terrible flood and I lost three of my good winter coats. Just completely ruined them with mildew. I had to throw them all out in big black garbage bags. \n\nBut... oh, what was I saying? Right, about enjoying things. Yes, I used to look forward to the watercolors, and my dance class, and going for walks with the ladies in the neighborhood. We used to walk the whole perimeter of the park together. Martha always tries to race everyone since she got her new hip, which is just silly at our age, but she means well. \n\nLately, though... I don't know. I just leave my painting things sitting right there on the table. The water cup just sits there drying out. I don't go to dance, and I don't go walking. I just can't seem to make myself care about any of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a moment ago, yes, I really have. I don't touch my paints or go to my classes. The interest is just... gone. I don't even care to go down to the grocery store much anymore, to be honest. \n\nI used to really like picking out the fresh fruit at that little market on the corner. They always had those lovely Georgia peaches in the summer. My mother used to make the most wonderful peach cobbler when I was a girl. She had this old cast iron skillet that weighed an absolute ton, and she'd bake the cobbler right in there. You had to use a special thick mitt just to take it out of the oven, otherwise you'd burn your hand right off. My brother actually did burn his hand on it once, had a blister the size of a quarter on his thumb for weeks. He was always getting into things he shouldn't, climbing fences and tearing the knees right out of his trousers. My mother was always patching them up with these little iron-on squares... \n\nBut, um, anyway... yes. I've lost interest in all of it. Just like I said.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about these thoughts of not wanting to wake up?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Well... yes. It just eats at me. You see, when I get so tired, or when I get annoyed with my aunt—which is happening more and more with all those boxes of hers in the way—I start thinking I'm just a bad woman. I really do. I feel like I'm bringing shame on my family for not being more grateful that we have each other. \n\nMy family was always very strict about gratitude. We had to write thank-you notes for absolutely everything when I was a girl. Even if it was just a plain pair of white socks from the Sears catalog. Do you remember how thick the Sears catalog used to be? You could use it as a booster seat. My mother actually did use it as a booster seat for my cousin Betty when she came to visit. Betty was always so small for her age. She ended up marrying a man who was nearly six foot four, if you can believe it! They looked so funny standing next to each other at the altar. He wore this awful mustard-colored tuxedo, which I suppose was popular back then, but it just clashed terribly with the bridesmaids' dresses. They were a lovely seafoam green, made of this shiny taffeta material that rustled every time you took a step. I always loved taffeta, even though it's the devil to iron... \n\nBut... oh dear, I'm rambling again, aren't I? What was the question? Oh, the guilt. Yes. I just feel so ashamed of myself for being so ungrateful about my aunt.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel pleasure when you do things that you used to enjoy, even if you don't feel like doing them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. Even if I force myself to sit down with my watercolors, it just feels like... a chore, I suppose. There's no spark there anymore. I used to get such a thrill when the colors blended just right on the paper, especially the blues and the greens. I had this one tube of cerulean blue that was just magnificent. I bought it at this little art supply shop downtown that's been closed for... oh, it must be five years now. The owner was a lovely man named Mr. Henderson. He always wore these little half-moon glasses perched right on the end of his nose, and he had a ginger cat that would sleep right on top of the cash register. It was the fattest cat I've ever seen in my life, just a massive thing. Mr. Henderson said it ate nothing but tuna fish and heavy cream, which I told him was terrible for the poor creature's heart, but he just laughed. He had a very deep, booming laugh that you wouldn't expect from such a small man. He actually reminded me a bit of my uncle Arthur, the one who used to play the accordion at all the family reunions...\n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry. No, I don't get any pleasure from it. Not the painting, not the dance class, not the walks. I just leave my things sitting there and I really can't make myself care about any of it, no matter how hard I try.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Well, my husband actually mentioned that the other day. He says I move like everything takes extra effort now. Even when I go to the market, I just... I stand there for the longest time before picking up what I came for. Like the other day, I was standing in front of the canned tomatoes. There used to be just two kinds, whole or crushed, but now they have diced, fire-roasted, with basil, with garlic... it's just too much. I remember when my grandmother used to can her own tomatoes. She had this huge garden out back, and in late August, her kitchen would just be covered in mason jars. She'd boil them in this enormous aluminum pot that took up two burners on the stove. The whole house smelled like vinegar and hot glass. She'd always make me write the labels because she said my handwriting was the neatest. I used to practice my cursive for hours in this little spiral notebook with a picture of a kitten on the front. I think I bought it at the Woolworth's that used to be on Main Street. They had a lunch counter there where you could get a grilled cheese sandwich and a cherry Coke for practically nothing. The lady who worked the counter was named Marge, and she always wore her hair in this towering beehive...\n\nBut, um, what were we saying? Oh, yes. Moving slowly. Yes, I suppose I do. Everything just feels so heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Weight? Oh, no, I haven't really noticed any change. My clothes still fit the same, I suppose. Though I haven't been dressing up much lately. I mostly just wear my old housecoats. I have this one flannel housecoat that I've had for... oh, it must be fifteen years. It's a pale yellow with little blue cornflowers on it. It used to be so soft, but it's gotten a bit scratchy from being washed so many times. I always wash it with that powdered detergent, the kind that comes in the big cardboard box. My mother always said powder was better than liquid, but she was very particular about laundry. She used to hang everything out on the line, even in the dead of winter. She said the freezing air made the sheets smell fresher. I remember helping her pin them up, and my fingers would get so numb I could barely squeeze the clothespins. We had those wooden clothespins, the ones with the little metal springs. My brother used to take them apart and use the springs to build these little catapults. He was always building things. He wanted to be an engineer, but then he ended up working at the post office... \n\nBut, no, my weight hasn't really changed. I just don't have much of an appetite, but I haven't lost any weight that I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose I do. Especially when it comes to my aunt. She needs a lot of help, and sometimes I just feel so tired, or I get annoyed with her. And then I start thinking I'm just a terrible, bad woman. I feel like I'm bringing shame on my whole family for not being more grateful and patient. Family is so important, you know. My aunt, she used to bake these incredible pies for every family gathering. Rhubarb, mostly, because she grew it right along the side of her garage. She had this huge patch of it, and the leaves were as big as elephant ears. I used to hide under them when I was a little girl playing hide-and-seek with my cousins. My cousin Tommy was always the seeker because he was the oldest, but he was terrible at it. He'd just wander around looking at bugs. He was obsessed with caterpillars. He used to keep them in these old mayonnaise jars with holes punched in the lid. He had this one big fuzzy one that he named Bartholomew... \n\nBut, oh dear, I'm wandering again. Yes, I just... I feel so guilty and worthless when I get frustrated with her. I should be better.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about actually doing something to make yourself not wake up?\n\nPatient: Doing something? Oh, no, I haven't tried to hurt myself. But... I do keep thinking it would be such a relief if I just didn't wake up at all. Honestly, that's what scared me enough to finally come in here to see you. Just the thought of staying asleep. I have this very old mattress, you see. It's one we bought back in nineteen-ninety-something. We had it delivered in this big truck, and the delivery men had such a time getting it up the stairs because our staircase has that sharp turn right at the landing. We have this little oak table on the landing where I keep a spider plant. Spider plants are so easy to take care of, they just shoot off those little babies on the stems. I used to snip them off and root them in little glass jars of water on the kitchen windowsill. My kitchen window faces east, so it gets all that bright morning light. The glass jars would catch the sun and make these little rainbows on the linoleum floor. Our old cat, Barnaby—he was a big orange tabby with a white patch on his nose—he used to chase those little rainbows for hours until he'd just tire himself out and fall asleep right there on the rug... \n\nWait, what was I... oh. Yes. I haven't actually tried to do anything. It just scared me that I wanted to just stay asleep like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or restless, or do you feel like you're moving in slow motion most of the time?\n\nPatient: Well, my husband says I move like everything just takes extra effort now. I don't feel restless, really, just... heavy, I suppose. He noticed it especially when we go to the market. He says I'll just stand there for entirely too long before picking up what I came for. We usually go to that little market down on 4th Street, the one with the faded green awning? They used to have the absolute best tomatoes in the summer, those big heavy beefsteak ones. My husband just loves a tomato sandwich with a little mayonnaise and black pepper on toasted white bread. We used to buy our bread from the bakery right next door to the market, but they closed down last spring. The owner, Mr. Abernathy, he finally retired and moved down to Florida to live with his daughter. She just had twins, a boy and a girl, and he was so excited to be a grandfather. He always wore these little flour-dusted aprons and he'd give the neighborhood kids free sugar cookies on Tuesdays... \n\nOh, excuse me, I'm rambling again. But yes, I suppose I do feel like I'm in slow motion. Just standing at the shelves, trying to gather the energy to reach out and grab a can of soup.\n\nDoctor: Have you made any plans for how you would make yourself not wake up?\n\nPatient: No, no plans. Like I was saying before... I haven't tried to hurt myself or figure out a way to do it. It's just that thought, that feeling that it would be such a relief to just... not wake up. And that's really what scared me enough to finally come in here to see you. Just the thought of it. Because usually, I'm the one taking care of everything, you know? I have to make sure my husband's shirts are pressed for church on Sundays. We go to the First Presbyterian over on Elm Street. It has those beautiful stained glass windows that they had restored back in 2005. They brought in these specialists all the way from Boston just to fix the lead lining. It took them three months, and during that time we had to hold services in the fellowship hall in the basement. The basement always smelled a little bit like old coffee and floor wax. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, he used this industrial wax that came in these big metal tins, and he'd buff the floors every Friday afternoon so they'd be shining for Sunday... \n\nOh, I'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? But no, to answer your question, no plans. Just that scary thought of wanting the relief of not waking up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not restless. Like I mentioned a moment ago, my husband says I move like everything takes extra effort now. I don't feel like I need to fidget or move around. It's more like... I'm just so heavy. Even at the market, I'll just stand there for entirely too long before picking up what I came for. It's like my arms are made of lead. Lead is such a heavy metal, isn't it? My father used to work in a factory that made those lead pipes for plumbing, back before they realized how dangerous it was. He'd come home smelling like hot metal and grease, and his hands were always so rough. He used this special pumice soap to wash up at the kitchen sink. It came in this little green tub and it had real grit in it, like sand. My mother hated it because it always left a ring around the porcelain, and she was so particular about keeping the sink sparkling white. She'd scrub it with baking soda and a little bit of vinegar. It would fizz up so nicely... \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, the restlessness. No, I don't feel restless at all. Just very, very slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. I know you've asked about that a few times now, but it's actually quite the opposite. Like I was telling you before, my husband always points out that I move like every little thing takes so much extra effort. He says I'm just so slow. Like when we're at the market, I'll just stand there staring at the shelves for entirely too long before I finally pick up what we need. We usually go on Tuesday mornings because they get their fresh produce deliveries on Mondays. They have this young boy who unloads the trucks, he can't be more than nineteen. He reminds me so much of my nephew, David. David was always such a hard worker, even when he was little he'd help his father chop wood out back. They had this beautiful old oak tree that got struck by lightning during a terrible storm back in '98, I think it was. The thunder was so loud it rattled the good china in my mother's hutch. That hutch was solid cherry wood, passed down from my grandmother... \n\nOh, I'm doing it again. I'm sorry. But no, no pacing or constant motion. Just very slow and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently that you've noticed?\n\nPatient: Weight? No, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any change in how my clothes fit or anything. I still wear the same dresses I've had for years. My favorite one is this navy blue floral print I bought for my niece's graduation back in... oh, it must have been 2012. She graduated from the state university with a degree in education. She's a second-grade teacher now, over at the elementary school on Maple Drive. They just built a new playground there last summer with those soft rubber mats underneath the swings instead of woodchips. Woodchips always seemed so dangerous to me, you know? You could get a splinter right through your shoe if you weren't careful. When I was a little girl, I got a terrible splinter in my heel from walking barefoot on our back porch. My mother had to use a sterilized needle to dig it out, and it stung so badly. She put iodine on it afterward, and it stained my skin orange for days...\n\nOh dear, I've wandered off again. But no, to answer your question, my weight has been about the same. I haven't lost any.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving at a normal pace when you're doing everyday activities?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not a normal pace. I think I've mentioned this a couple times now, but my husband says I move like everything takes extra effort. Like I'm just so slow. Even at the market, I'll just stand there entirely too long before I finally pick up what I came for. The market gets so chilly, too, right in that produce section. They keep the air conditioning blasting over the lettuce to keep it crisp, I suppose. I always have to bring a cardigan. I have this lovely cream-colored one I knitted myself, took me almost a year to finish because my arthritis was acting up so badly that winter. The yarn was a blend of merino wool and silk, very soft, but quite expensive. I bought it at that little craft store downtown, the one that used to be a bakery. You can still smell the yeast and vanilla sometimes if you go in early enough. They used to make the most wonderful bear claws there, with real almond paste... \n\nOh, I'm sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? But no, I don't feel like I'm moving at a normal pace at all. Just very slow and heavy.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Fidgeting? No, no. Like I was saying, it's really the exact opposite. I just sit there, heavy as a stone. I spend most of the day just sitting in my recliner in the living room. Betty was always so small for her age. She ended up marrying a man who was nearly six foot four, if you can believe it! They looked so funny standing next to each other at the altar. She had this huge garden out back, and in late August, her kitchen would just be covered in mason jars. She'd boil them in this enormous aluminum pot that took up two burners on the stove. The whole house smelled like vinegar and hot glass. She'd always make me write the labels because she said my handwriting was the neatest. I used to practice my cursive for hours in this little spiral notebook with a picture of a kitten on the front. I think I bought it at the Woolworth's that used to be on Main Street. Our old cat, Barnaby—he was a big orange tabby with a white patch on his nose—he used to chase those little rainbows for hours until he'd just tire himself out and fall asleep right there on the rug... \n\nWait, what was I... oh. It just scared me that I wanted to just stay asleep like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or restless, or do you feel like you're moving in slow motion most of the time?\n\nPatient: Well, my husband says I move like everything just takes extra effort now. I don't feel restless, really, just... heavy, I suppose. He noticed it especially when we go to the market. He says I'll just stand there for entirely too long before picking up what I came for. We usually go to that little market down on 4th Street, the one with the faded green awning? They used to have the absolute best tomatoes in the summer, those big heavy beefsteak ones. My husband just loves a tomato sandwich with a little mayonnaise and black pepper on toasted white bread. We used to buy our bread from the bakery right next door to the market, but they closed down last spring. The owner, Mr. Abernathy, he finally retired and moved down to Florida to live with his daughter. She just had twins, a boy and a girl, and he was so excited to be a grandfather. He always wore these little flour-dusted aprons and he'd give the neighborhood kids free sugar cookies on Tuesdays... \n\nOh, excuse me, I'm rambling again. But yes, I suppose I do feel like I'm in slow motion. Just standing at the shelves, trying to gather the energy to reach out and grab a can of soup.\n\nDoctor: Have you made any plans for how you would make yourself not wake up?\n\nPatient: No, no plans. Like I was saying before... I haven't tried to hurt myself or figure out a way to do it. It's just that thought, that feeling that it would be such a relief to just... not wake up. And that's really what scared me enough to finally come in here to see you. Just the thought of it. Because usually, I'm the one taking care of everything, you know? I have to make sure my husband's shirts are pressed for church on Sundays. We go to the First Presbyterian over on Elm Street. It has those beautiful stained glass windows that they had restored back in 2005. They brought in these specialists all the way from Boston just to fix the lead lining. It took them three months, and during that time we had to hold services in the fellowship hall in the basement. The basement always smelled a little bit like old coffee and floor wax. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, he used this industrial wax that came in these big metal tins, and he'd buff the floors every Friday afternoon so they'd be shining for Sunday... \n\nOh, I'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? But no, to answer your question, no plans. Just that scary thought of wanting the relief of not waking up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not restless. Like I mentioned a moment ago, my husband says I move like everything takes extra effort now. I don't feel like I need to fidget or move around. It's more like... I'm just so heavy. Even at the market, I'll just stand there for entirely too long before picking up what I came for. It's like my arms are made of lead. Lead is such a heavy metal, isn't it? My father used to work in a factory that made those lead pipes for plumbing, back before they realized how dangerous it was. He'd come home smelling like hot metal and grease, and his hands were always so rough. He used this special pumice soap to wash up at the kitchen sink. It came in this little green tub and it had real grit in it, like sand. My mother hated it because it always left a ring around the porcelain, and she was so particular about keeping the sink sparkling white. She'd scrub it with baking soda and a little bit of vinegar. It would fizz up so nicely... \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, the restlessness. No, I don't feel restless at all. Just very, very slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. I know you've asked about that a few times now, but it's actually quite the opposite. Like I was telling you before, my husband always points out that I move like every little thing takes so much extra effort. He says I'm just so slow. Like when we're at the market, I'll just stand there staring at the shelves for entirely too long before I finally pick up what we need. We usually go on Tuesday mornings because they get their fresh produce deliveries on Mondays. They have this young boy who unloads the trucks, he can't be more than nineteen. He reminds me so much of my nephew, David. David was always such a hard worker, even when he was little he'd help his father chop wood out back. They had this beautiful old oak tree that got struck by lightning during a terrible storm back in '98, I think it was. The thunder was so loud it rattled the good china in my mother's hutch. That hutch was solid cherry wood, passed down from my grandmother... \n\nOh, I'm doing it again. I'm sorry. But no, no pacing or constant motion. Just very slow and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently that you've noticed?\n\nPatient: Weight? No, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any change in how my clothes fit or anything. I still wear the same dresses I've had for years. My favorite one is this navy blue floral print I bought for my niece's graduation back in... oh, it must have been 2012. She graduated from the state university with a degree in education. She's a second-grade teacher now, over at the elementary school on Maple Drive. They just built a new playground there last summer with those soft rubber mats underneath the swings instead of woodchips. Woodchips always seemed so dangerous to me, you know? You could get a splinter right through your shoe if you weren't careful. When I was a little girl, I got a terrible splinter in my heel from walking barefoot on our back porch. My mother had to use a sterilized needle to dig it out, and it stung so badly. She put iodine on it afterward, and it stained my skin orange for days...\n\nOh dear, I've wandered off again. But no, to answer your question, my weight has been about the same. I haven't lost any.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving at a normal pace when you're doing everyday activities?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not a normal pace. I think I've mentioned this a couple times now, but my husband says I move like everything takes extra effort. Like I'm just so slow. Even at the market, I'll just stand there entirely too long before I finally pick up what I came for. The market gets so chilly, too, right in that produce section. They keep the air conditioning blasting over the lettuce to keep it crisp, I suppose. I always have to bring a cardigan. I have this lovely cream-colored one I knitted myself, took me almost a year to finish because my arthritis was acting up so badly that winter. The yarn was a blend of merino wool and silk, very soft, but quite expensive. I bought it at that little craft store downtown, the one that used to be a bakery. You can still smell the yeast and vanilla sometimes if you go in early enough. They used to make the most wonderful bear claws there, with real almond paste... \n\nOh, I'm sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? But no, I don't feel like I'm moving at a normal pace at all. Just very slow and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling an intense need to move around when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Fidgeting? No, no. Like I was saying, it's really the exact opposite. I just sit there, heavy as a stone. I spend most of the day just sitting in my recliner in the living room. It's a brown corduroy one my husband bought me for our anniversary years ago. Corduroy is so terrible to keep clean, though. If you spill even a drop of tea on it, it just sinks right into those little ridges and leaves a dark spot. I drink a lot of chamomile tea in the afternoons. I just like the taste with a little spoonful of honey. We used to get our honey from a local man, Mr. Henderson, down the road, but he sold his hives a few years back. He said the bees were just getting too difficult to keep up with because of his bad knees. My knees aren't what they used to be either, to be honest, which is probably why I don't mind just sitting in that chair for hours... \n\nOh, dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I just get so sidetracked today. But no, no fidgeting. I don't feel the need to move around at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Well, that's... that's actually what scared me enough to come in and see you today. I haven't tried to hurt myself or anything like that, but I keep thinking it would just be such a relief if I went to sleep and didn't wake up. I've always been a good sleeper, you know. Even when I was a little girl, my mother used to say I could sleep through a thunderstorm. And we had some terrible storms back in Ohio where I grew up. The sky would turn this awful greenish-purple color right before the tornadoes came. We had a root cellar we'd hide in, smelled like damp earth and those old potatoes my father kept down there. He grew Russets mostly, said they were the best for baking. We'd have baked potatoes with just a little bit of butter and salt for supper sometimes when money was tight. Salt was cheaper back then, in those big blue cardboard canisters...

Oh, I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. But yes, to answer your question, I haven't made any plans or tried anything. I just... I just keep thinking about how nice it would be to just not wake up.

I don't even care to go down to the grocery store much anymore, to be honest. \n\nI used to really like picking out the fresh fruit at that little market on the corner. She had this old cast iron skillet that weighed an absolute ton, and she'd bake the cobbler right in there. I feel like I'm bringing shame on my family for not being more grateful that we have each other. \n\nMy family was always very strict about gratitude. We had to write thank-you notes for absolutely everything when I was a girl. Even if it was just a plain pair of white socks from the Sears catalog. Do you remember how thick the Sears catalog used to be? You could use it as a booster seat. My mother actually did use it as a booster seat for my cousin Betty when she came to visit. Betty was always so small for her age. She ended up marrying a man who was nearly six foot four, if you can believe it! They looked so funny standing next to each other at the altar. He wore this awful mustard-colored tuxedo, which I suppose was popular back then, but it just clashed terribly with the bridesmaids' dresses. They were a lovely seafoam green, made of this shiny taffeta material that rustled every time you took a step. I always loved taffeta, even though it's the devil to iron... \n\nBut... oh dear, I'm rambling again, aren't I? What was the question? Oh, the guilt. Yes. I just feel so ashamed of myself for being so ungrateful about my aunt.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel pleasure when you do things that you used to enjoy, even if you don't feel like doing them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I used to get such a thrill when the colors blended just right on the paper, especially the blues and the greens. I had this one tube of cerulean blue that was just magnificent. I bought it at this little art supply shop downtown that's been closed for... oh, it must be five years now. The owner was a lovely man named Mr. Henderson. He always wore these little half-moon glasses perched right on the end of his nose, and he had a ginger cat that would sleep right on top of the cash register. It was the fattest cat I've ever seen in my life, just a massive thing. He had a very deep, booming laugh that you wouldn't expect from such a small man. Like the other day, I was standing in front of the canned tomatoes. There used to be just two kinds, whole or crushed, but now they have diced, fire-roasted, with basil, with garlic... it's just too much. I remember when my grandmother used to can her own tomatoes. She had this huge garden out back, and in late August, her kitchen would just be covered in mason jars. She'd boil them in this enormous aluminum pot that took up two burners on the stove. The whole house smelled like vinegar and hot glass. She'd always make me write the labels because she said my handwriting was the neatest. I used to practice my cursive for hours in this little spiral notebook with a picture of a kitten on the front. I think I bought it at the Woolworth's that used to be on Main Street. They had a lunch counter there where you could get a grilled cheese sandwich and a cherry Coke for practically nothing. The lady who worked the counter was named Marge, and she always wore her hair in this towering beehive...\n\nBut, um, what were we saying? Oh, yes. Moving slowly. Yes, I suppose I do. Everything just feels so heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Weight? Oh, no, I haven't really noticed any change. I have this one flannel housecoat that I've had for... oh, it must be fifteen years. It's a pale yellow with little blue cornflowers on it. It used to be so soft, but it's gotten a bit scratchy from being washed so many times. I always wash it with that powdered detergent, the kind that comes in the big cardboard box. My mother always said powder was better than liquid, but she was very particular about laundry. She used to hang everything out on the line, even in the dead of winter. She said the freezing air made the sheets smell fresher. I remember helping her pin them up, and my fingers would get so numb I could barely squeeze the clothespins. We had those wooden clothespins, the ones with the little metal springs. My brother used to take them apart and use the springs to build these little catapults. He was always building things. The glass jars would catch the sun and make these little rainbows on the linoleum floor. We used to buy our bread from the bakery right next door to the market, but they closed down last spring. The owner, Mr. Abernathy, he finally retired and moved down to Florida to live with his daughter. She just had twins, a boy and a girl, and he was so excited to be a grandfather. He always wore these little flour-dusted aprons and he'd give the neighborhood kids free sugar cookies on Tuesdays... \n\nOh, excuse me, I'm rambling again. But yes, I suppose I do feel like I'm in slow motion. Just standing at the shelves, trying to gather the energy to reach out and grab a can of soup.\n\nDoctor: Have you made any plans for how you would make yourself not wake up?\n\nPatient: No, no plans. Like I was saying before... I haven't tried to hurt myself or figure out a way to do it. It's just that thought, that feeling that it would be such a relief to just... not wake up. And that's really what scared me enough to finally come in here to see you. Just the thought of it. Because usually, I'm the one taking care of everything, you know? I have to make sure my husband's shirts are pressed for church on Sundays. We go to the First Presbyterian over on Elm Street. It has those beautiful stained glass windows that they had restored back in 2005. They brought in these specialists all the way from Boston just to fix the lead lining. It took them three months, and during that time we had to hold services in the fellowship hall in the basement. The basement always smelled a little bit like old coffee and floor wax. The janitor, Mr. Henderson, he used this industrial wax that came in these big metal tins, and he'd buff the floors every Friday afternoon so they'd be shining for Sunday... \n\nOh, I'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? But no, to answer your question, no plans. Just that scary thought of wanting the relief of not waking up.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not restless. Like I mentioned a moment ago, my husband says I move like everything takes extra effort now. I don't feel like I need to fidget or move around. It's more like... I'm just so heavy. Even at the market, I'll just stand there for entirely too long before picking up what I came for. It's like my arms are made of lead. Lead is such a heavy metal, isn't it? My father used to work in a factory that made those lead pipes for plumbing, back before they realized how dangerous it was. He'd come home smelling like hot metal and grease, and his hands were always so rough. He used this special pumice soap to wash up at the kitchen sink. It came in this little green tub and it had real grit in it, like sand. My mother hated it because it always left a ring around the porcelain, and she was so particular about keeping the sink sparkling white. She'd scrub it with baking soda and a little bit of vinegar. It would fizz up so nicely... \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, the restlessness. No, I don't feel restless at all. Just very, very slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. I know you've asked about that a few times now, but it's actually quite the opposite. Like I was telling you before, my husband always points out that I move like every little thing takes so much extra effort. He says I'm just so slow. Like when we're at the market, I'll just stand there staring at the shelves for entirely too long before I finally pick up what we need. We usually go on Tuesday mornings because they get their fresh produce deliveries on Mondays. They have this young boy who unloads the trucks, he can't be more than nineteen. He reminds me so much of my nephew, David. David was always such a hard worker, even when he was little he'd help his father chop wood out back. They had this beautiful old oak tree that got struck by lightning during a terrible storm back in '98, I think it was. The thunder was so loud it rattled the good china in my mother's hutch. That hutch was solid cherry wood, passed down from my grandmother... \n\nOh, I'm doing it again. I'm sorry. But no, no pacing or constant motion. Just very slow and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently that you've noticed?\n\nPatient: Weight? No, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any change in how my clothes fit or anything. I still wear the same dresses I've had for years. My favorite one is this navy blue floral print I bought for my niece's graduation back in... oh, it must have been 2012. She graduated from the state university with a degree in education. She's a second-grade teacher now, over at the elementary school on Maple Drive. They just built a new playground there last summer with those soft rubber mats underneath the swings instead of woodchips. Woodchips always seemed so dangerous to me, you know? You could get a splinter right through your shoe if you weren't careful. When I was a little girl, I got a terrible splinter in my heel from walking barefoot on our back porch. My mother had to use a sterilized needle to dig it out, and it stung so badly. She put iodine on it afterward, and it stained my skin orange for days...\n\nOh dear, I've wandered off again. But no, to answer your question, my weight has been about the same. I haven't lost any.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving at a normal pace when you're doing everyday activities?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not a normal pace. I think I've mentioned this a couple times now, but my husband says I move like everything takes extra effort. Like I'm just so slow. Even at the market, I'll just stand there entirely too long before I finally pick up what I came for. The market gets so chilly, too, right in that produce section. They keep the air conditioning blasting over the lettuce to keep it crisp, I suppose. I always have to bring a cardigan. I have this lovely cream-colored one I knitted myself, took me almost a year to finish because my arthritis was acting up so badly that winter. The yarn was a blend of merino wool and silk, very soft, but quite expensive. I bought it at that little craft store downtown, the one that used to be a bakery. You can still smell the yeast and vanilla sometimes if you go in early enough. They used to make the most wonderful bear claws there, with real almond paste... \n\nOh, I'm sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? But no, I don't feel like I'm moving at a normal pace at all. Just very slow and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling an intense need to move around when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Fidgeting? No, no. Like I was saying, it's really the exact opposite. I just sit there, heavy as a stone. I spend most of the day just sitting in my recliner in the living room. It's a brown corduroy one my husband bought me for our anniversary years ago. Corduroy is so terrible to keep clean, though. If you spill even a drop of tea on it, it just sinks right into those little ridges and leaves a dark spot. I drink a lot of chamomile tea in the afternoons. I just like the taste with a little spoonful of honey. We used to get our honey from a local man, Mr. Henderson, down the road, but he sold his hives a few years back. He said the bees were just getting too difficult to keep up with because of his bad knees. My knees aren't what they used to be either, to be honest, which is probably why I don't mind just sitting in that chair for hours... \n\nOh, dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I just get so sidetracked today. But no, no fidgeting. I don't feel the need to move around at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would make yourself not wake up?\n\nPatient: Well, that's... that's actually what scared me enough to come in and see you today. I haven't tried to hurt myself or anything like that, but I keep thinking it would just be such a relief if I went to sleep and didn't wake up. I've always been a good sleeper, you know. Even when I was a little girl, my mother used to say I could sleep through a thunderstorm. And we had some terrible storms back in Ohio where I grew up. The sky would turn this awful greenish-purple color right before the tornadoes came. We had a root cellar we'd hide in, smelled like damp earth and those old potatoes my father kept down there. He grew Russets mostly, said they were the best for baking. We'd have baked potatoes with just a little bit of butter and salt for supper sometimes when money was tight. Salt was cheaper back then, in those big blue cardboard canisters...\n\nOh, I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. But yes, to answer your question, I haven't made any plans or tried anything. I just... I just keep thinking about how nice it would be to just not wake up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, not at all. Like I said before, it's really the opposite. My husband keeps telling me I move like everything takes so much extra effort now. Even when I go to the market, I'll just stand there entirely too long before I finally pick up what I came for. The market is just so bright, you know? I didn't finish, of course, left in the 11th grade to help my mother when she got sick, but I still remember those awful buzzing lights in the hallways. The floors were always so shiny, though. The janitor, Mr. Higgins, he used this wax that smelled like lemons and old wood. He was a very kind man, always had a peppermint in his pocket if you looked sad. Peppermints are too sharp for me now, though. They hurt my teeth...

The radiator there used to clank all night long—just bang, bang, bang. I asked the landlord to fix it, oh, a dozen times, but he never did. He was a cheap man, always wearing those same scuffed brown shoes. I remember thinking I should just learn to fix it myself, but I never was much good at mechanical things after I left school. Poodles and terriers with these tiny, painted-on eyes that always seem to be staring right at you. I used to just love my watercolors, I really did. I'd always look forward to getting them out. And heavens, it has been raining so much this month. The man on the local news said it's the wettest spring we've had in a decade. I always worry about the basement flooding when it rains like this, because back in the late nineties we had a terrible flood and I lost three of my good winter coats. Just completely ruined them with mildew. I had to throw them all out in big black garbage bags. \n\nBut... oh, what was I saying? Right, about enjoying things. Yes, I used to look forward to the watercolors, and my dance class, and going for walks with the ladies in the neighborhood. We used to walk the whole perimeter of the park together. I just leave my painting things sitting right there on the table. The water cup just sits there drying out. I don't go to dance, and I don't go walking. I just can't seem to make myself care about any of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a moment ago, yes, I really have. I don't touch my paints or go to my classes. The interest is just... gone. I don't even care to go down to the grocery store much anymore, to be honest. \n\nI used to really like picking out the fresh fruit at that little market on the corner. They always had those lovely Georgia peaches in the summer. My mother used to make the most wonderful peach cobbler when I was a girl. She had this old cast iron skillet that weighed an absolute ton, and she'd bake the cobbler right in there. You had to use a special thick mitt just to take it out of the oven, otherwise you'd burn your hand right off. My brother actually did burn his hand on it once, had a blister the size of a quarter on his thumb for weeks. He was always getting into things he shouldn't, climbing fences and tearing the knees right out of his trousers. My mother was always patching them up with these little iron-on squares... \n\nBut, um, anyway... yes. I've lost interest in all of it. I feel like I'm bringing shame on my family for not being more grateful that we have each other. \n\nMy family was always very strict about gratitude. We had to write thank-you notes for absolutely everything when I was a girl. Even if it was just a plain pair of white socks from the Sears catalog. Do you remember how thick the Sears catalog used to be? You could use it as a booster seat. My mother actually did use it as a booster seat for my cousin Betty when she came to visit. Betty was always so small for her age. She ended up marrying a man who was nearly six foot four, if you can believe it! They looked so funny standing next to each other at the altar. He wore this awful mustard-colored tuxedo, which I suppose was popular back then, but it just clashed terribly with the bridesmaids' dresses. They were a lovely seafoam green, made of this shiny taffeta material that rustled every time you took a step. I always loved taffeta, even though it's the devil to iron... \n\nBut... oh dear, I'm rambling again, aren't I? What was the question? Oh, the guilt. Yes. I just feel so ashamed of myself for being so ungrateful about my aunt.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel pleasure when you do things that you used to enjoy, even if you don't feel like doing them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. Even if I force myself to sit down with my watercolors, it just feels like... a chore, I suppose. There's no spark there anymore. I used to get such a thrill when the colors blended just right on the paper, especially the blues and the greens. I had this one tube of cerulean blue that was just magnificent. I bought it at this little art supply shop downtown that's been closed for... oh, it must be five years now. The owner was a lovely man named Mr. Henderson. He always wore these little half-moon glasses perched right on the end of his nose, and he had a ginger cat that would sleep right on top of the cash register. It was the fattest cat I've ever seen in my life, just a massive thing. Mr. Henderson said it ate nothing but tuna fish and heavy cream, which I told him was terrible for the poor creature's heart, but he just laughed. He had a very deep, booming laugh that you wouldn't expect from such a small man. He actually reminded me a bit of my uncle Arthur, the one who used to play the accordion at all the family reunions...\n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry. No, I don't get any pleasure from it. Not the painting, not the dance class, not the walks. I just leave my things sitting there and I really can't make myself care about any of it, no matter how hard I try.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Well, my husband actually mentioned that the other day. He says I move like everything takes extra effort now. Even when I go to the market, I just... I stand there for the longest time before picking up what I came for. Like the other day, I was standing in front of the canned tomatoes. There used to be just two kinds, whole or crushed, but now they have diced, fire-roasted, with basil, with garlic... it's just too much. I remember when my grandmother used to can her own tomatoes. She had this huge garden out back, and in late August, her kitchen would just be covered in mason jars. She'd boil them in this enormous aluminum pot that took up two burners on the stove. The whole house smelled like vinegar and hot glass. She'd always make me write the labels because she said my handwriting was the neatest. I used to practice my cursive for hours in this little spiral notebook with a picture of a kitten on the front. I think I bought it at the Woolworth's that used to be on Main Street. They had a lunch counter there where you could get a grilled cheese sandwich and a cherry Coke for practically nothing. The lady who worked the counter was named Marge, and she always wore her hair in this towering beehive...\n\nBut, um, what were we saying? Oh, yes. Moving slowly. Yes, I suppose I do. Everything just feels so heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Weight? Oh, no, I haven't really noticed any change. My clothes still fit the same, I suppose. Though I haven't been dressing up much lately. I mostly just wear my old housecoats. I have this one flannel housecoat that I've had for... oh, it must be fifteen years. It's a pale yellow with little blue cornflowers on it. It used to be so soft, but it's gotten a bit scratchy from being washed so many times. I always wash it with that powdered detergent, the kind that comes in the big cardboard box. My mother always said powder was better than liquid, but she was very particular about laundry. She used to hang everything out on the line, even in the dead of winter. She said the freezing air made the sheets smell fresher. I remember helping her pin them up, and my fingers would get so numb I could barely squeeze the clothespins. We had those wooden clothespins, the ones with the little metal springs. My brother used to take them apart and use the springs to build these little catapults. He was always building things. He wanted to be an engineer, but then he ended up working at the post office... \n\nBut, no, my weight hasn't really changed. I just don't have much of an appetite, but I haven't lost any weight that I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose I do. Especially when it comes to my aunt. She needs a lot of help, and sometimes I just feel so tired, or I get annoyed with her. And then I start thinking I'm just a terrible, bad woman. I feel like I'm bringing shame on my whole family for not being more grateful and patient. Family is so important, you know. My aunt, she used to bake these incredible pies for every family gathering. Rhubarb, mostly, because she grew it right along the side of her garage. She had this huge patch of it, and the leaves were as big as elephant ears. I used to hide under them when I was a little girl playing hide-and-seek with my cousins. My cousin Tommy was always the seeker because he was the oldest, but he was terrible at it. He'd just wander around looking at bugs. He was obsessed with caterpillars. He used to keep them in these old mayonnaise jars with holes punched in the lid. He had this one big fuzzy one that he named Bartholomew... \n\nBut, oh dear, I'm wandering again. Yes, I just...
